# A NEW APPROACH TO RAIN FORECASTING (Secular Changes In Teleconnections: Causes & Remedies)

**Author:** Vinay Jha (KSDSU / Darbhanga, India)
**Contact:** vinayjhaa@gmail.com

bjbj

A     NEW     APPROACH     TO     RAIN     FORECASTING

( Secular  Changes  In  Teleconnections  :  Causes  &  Remedies )

Vinay Jha

Near MRM College North Gate, KSDSU  Post Office, PIN-846008, India.   (e-mail :

HYPERLINK "mailto:vinayjhaa@gmail.com"

vinayjhaa@gmail.com

)

ABSTRACT: Spectral analysis of past data reveals a 61-year cycle in Indian and global rainfall, NAO (North Atlantic Oscillation), North Atlantic SST (Sea Surface Temperature), El Ni

o, Darwin pressure, etc. Waveform of annual Indian rainfall almost replicates that of 61 years ago for nearly half of this cycle, and this half-cycle also exhibits strong coupling between Indian monsoon rainfall and its predictors, the other half-cycle lasting about three decades being rather chaotic for which neither any of the existing forecasting methods nor this 61-year cycle delivers tolerable predictions. The predicand (rainfall) as well as physical predictors are both guided by this common 61-year mysterious cycle which cannot be explained on the basis of hitherto known physical processes. Comparison with waveforms distanced 122 years suggest that this 61-year cycle may be a stochastic manifestation of some unknown annual process which may throw light on the real causes hidden underneath the apparent chaos of climatic systems.

If we organise relevant data and forecasts according to the climatic year beginning from mid-April, this 61-year cycle shows a better contrast between chaotic and predictable phases, and standard deviation of spectra 61 years apart also gets diminished. Since delinking and relinking of teleconnected phenomena also occur at the beginning of this climatic year, some mysterious phenomenon may be said to occur at the beginning of each climatic year in April, which influences the overall annual performance of rainfall and related phenomena. Physical conditions in April or May may sometimes fail to predict events in June, such as in 1997 when En Ni

o failed to influence ISMR (Indian Summer Monsoon Rainfall) or in 2002 when Indian drought could not be predicted, while the waveforms of 61 years ago were replicated even during these unusual years. Thus, some long range regularity overrides short range vagaries of monsoons.

In addition to the precaution needed in keeping time frame in proper order, geographical distribution of teleconnections and the magnitude of their mutual correlations must be up to date too. Geographical distribution of annual relative rainfall suggests a phenomena which may be described in terms of Climatic Meridians, with poles at Mt Kenya and Mid-Pacific, in reference to which correlations between teleconnections can be understood properly . An annual fluctuation of upto a maximum limit of 17 degrees in these climatic latitudes, occurring in mid-April, gives rise to secular change in the magnitudes and often signs of correlation coefficients . If these precautions are followed carefully, a highly reliable annual rain forecast beginning from mid-April can be made for the whole world.

1.   61-YEAR   CLIMATIC   CYCLE

Researchers have noted a quasi-60 year cycle in the variability of ISMR (as well as of Chinese monsoons ), with two ~30-year  phases of above and  below  normal  performance of ISMR 1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9.  Hastenrath and Parthasarathy et al think that data length of 30-years is necessary and sufficient to establish a stable correlation for prediction purposes. Their opinion was based on the observations over a period which was comparatively a more predictable period according to the results of spectral analysis presented by us below. Parthasarathy and Mooley carried correlogram and spectrum analysis of 108-year series and noticed that smaller cycles of 14 and 2.8 years had developed within a 30-year period. Long-term variability in the correlation of ISMR with several of its predictors have been studied by many researchers . We have found that these multi-year trends show periodicities which randomly differ from that deduced from an analysis of annual rainfall indices, but the present study is focussed on annual variability of annual rainfall and of its predictors on account of special significance of annual variability.

Mooley and Parthasarthy 10  and Parthasarathy et. al. 11, 12, 13  derived time-series of all India average rainfall on seasonal and monthly time-scales as a weighted average of the data at 306 stations obtained from the IMD ( Indian Meteorological Department ). We used it to carry out some simple time series analyses. Departure of ISMR from long term average in  percents  is shown in the Fig-1, in which  data of 136 years are shown in two series,1871-1945 (years being numbered  from 1 to 75 along x-axis shown in red) and 1932-2006 (years being numbered  from 62 to 136 along x-axis shown in blue). For instance, 1957 AD is numbered  87 (= 1957 - 1870 ) and will be found in the second series in blue . When the second curve was superimposed upon the first one, shape of both waveforms coincided in a majority of cases when the gap between both series was chosen to be 61 years. Waveform of 1891-1923 ( year nos. 21-53 ) had an similarity in shape with that of 1952-1984 ( year numbers 82-114) in 25 out of 33 years , only 8 years were out of phase . Similarly, Since 1950-2006 , only 19 years had out of phase waveforms out of 57 years . Only the shape of wave is copied after 61 years, and not the magnitude (amplitude). There are long periods when shape (but not the amplitude) of the waveform of 61 years ago was copied to a large extent, followed by periods when this conformity is much less marked, bordering on chaos.

Since the onset  and withdrawal of summer monsoon does not have a definite date , data organised according to four calendar months of June-September does not give us a realistic idea about the seasonal quantum of ISMR . Sometimes significant portions of ISMR occur in May and October too . For instance, the worst year for ISMR in recorded history was 1877, when June-September rainfall was -28.3% deficient . But if May and October are also included in 1877  ISMR, deficit would reduce to -19.9% and 1877 will lose its place as the worst year in recorded history. There is a difference of 8.4% between  -28.3% and -19.9% , which is significant . Artificiality of calendar months is the main cause behind failures to discriminate signal from noise in the time series data processing of Indian monsoons . Total annual rainfall in India is a better index than ISMR if we want to analyse total quanta of hydrological exchanges between land and ocean in the larger context of land-sea-interaction . Waveform comparisons of total annual rainfall with that of 61 years ago produce a clearer picture : the contrast between weak and strong correlation periods is more faithfully represented by total annual rainfall series than by ISMR series, and standard deviation between two series 61 years apart has also decreased which is evident from the closer approximation both waveforms make to each other . FIG - 2 shows annual spectral comparisons . Blue shade shows strongly coupled (predictable) and white shade shows weakly coupled (chaotic) phases during 1871-2005.

B N Goswami rightly says

The predictability of the Indian summer monsoon may also undergo interdecadal variability

1  . M. Raajeevan observed :

(The) relationship ( between the predictors and ISMR ) was weak in 1930s and 1940s and during the recent years. During the 1960s to 1990s this relationship was, however,very strong. Obviously, the skill of the model also shows similar type of variations.

14 .What M. Raajeevan says about the relation of ISMR and its physical predictors applies equally well to the relation between annual Indian rainfall value of any given year with that of 61 years ago . When this waveform shows poor resemblance with that of 61 years ago, correlation coefficients of physical predictors are also poor . And when predictors behave more rationally, annual Indian rainfall waveform almost copies the waveform of 61 years ago ! Not a mere coincidence !

1874-1895 is the chaotic part and 1896-1934 is the predictable  part of first 61-year cycle (1874-1934) during the analysed period 1871-2005. As expected, 1935-1956 is the chaotic part of next 61-year cycle (1935-1995). But the the predictable part of next 61-year cycle  does not appear to be confined to 1957-1986, because the initial part of the next (third) 61-year cycle (1987-2047) is not chaotic as the patterns of preceding two cycles would have suggested. As shown in Fig-3a and Fig-3b, annual rainfall waveform of 1993-2003 (actually upto 2006, with the sole exception of 2004 during these 14 years) copies the waveform of 61 years ago, but this period ought to have fallen in the chaotic phase, which should occur during 1995-2016, of this 61-year period (1995-2055). Moreover, the waveform of 1993-2006 conforms to that of one cycle ago (i.e., 61 years ago) but does not conform to that of two cycles ago, as shown in Fig-3a and Fig-3b . Fig-3b shows the ISMR waveforms of more than three full cycles since 1813, in addition to projections of annual Indian rainfall for 2007-2036 climatic years beginning from mid-April based upon our new type of forecasting model . It suggests that this 61-year cycle does not always repeat itself in the same manner and there may be longer periodicities hidden in it which we are not able to uncover due to lack of many centuries of data. This unexpectedly predictable behaviour of the 1993-2006 rain graph makes it clear that we cannot rely upon this 61-year cycle for making forecasts in a simplistic manner, because over centuries the pattern of this cycle itself changes and one cannot predict whether we have to face a predictable or a chaotic part of the 61-year cycle in future. But the fact is undeniable that this 61-year cycle actually exists, because waveform replication for many years and even decades is too frequent to be ignored as a chance affair.

Without using any physical predictor, we have obtained nearly same results about 6-decade periodicity of Indian Monsoon which many researchers had noticed in the case of physical predictors. There are periods of strong coupling of annual rainfall spectrum with that of 61 years ago, preceded and followed by periods of weak coupling . Timings of these periods of strong and weak couplings also coincide with those obtained from analysis of time series of correlation coefficients (CCs) between rainfall and its physical predictors . It is a strange conclusion, which suggests a redundancy of all physical parameters ! If rainfall waveforms of 61 years ago are used as the sole predictor, we can make equally good (or bad) forecasts ( it is, however, not advisable) ! For instance, 1941 drought was exactly reflected in 2002, and the difference in magnitudes of rainfall was also nearly equal to the trend rate, while all modelers were predicting a normal monsoon in 2002 . Same is the case with 1997, when En Ni

o failed to serve as a good predictor, while waveform of 61 years back was admirably replicated . Meteorological wisdom is that the accuracy of forecasts decreases if range is increased . But in cases like those of 1997 and 2002 ISMR, we witnessed the opposite : while physical conditions of April failed to predict events of June, certain unknown conditions of 61-years ago seemed to cause such changes in physical conditions during May so as to create a drought in June-July of 2002 ! Entire waveform of 1894-1924 is almost copied by 1957-1985 rainfall spectrum ( Fig- 2), with just 4 exceptions during 29 years ( down from 8 exceptions during the same period in the case of ISMR, which is a proof of the fact that annual rainfall index is a better indicator of this long-term periodicity than ISMR ) . Such a behaviour for 3 decades cannot be a mere coincidence. But while emphasizing the importance of this 61-year cycle, it must not be forgotten that it may deceive us too, especially during the chaotic period .

When annual South Oscillation Index (SOI) for 1876-2006 (Darwin-Tahiti) in two series distanced 61 years apart are superimposed upon each other, no trace of 61-year cycle is seen . Correlation of ENSO ( El Ni

o and South Oscillation ) with ISMR 14, however, suggests that ENSO also ought to have a periodicity similar to that witnessed in the case of ISMR . ISMR and its correlation with ENSO show same long term periodicities , but  Darwin-Tahiti SO fails to show such a 61-year cyclicity . Pressure data for Darwin (from

HYPERLINK "http://www.cru.uea.ac.uk/ftpdata/soi_dar.dat"

http://www.cru.uea.ac.uk/ftpdata/soi_dar.dat

shown in Fig-6) , however, shows a similar 61-year cycle. Clearly, Darwin-Tahiti SO ought to be replaced with some other type of SO in order to find a better index for predicting ISMR . ISMR-ENSO correlation is substantial but incomplete . A more appropriate type of SO may enhance the correlation of ENSO with ISMR . Another possible explanation of this incomplete correlation is the chaotic half of 61-year cycle which reduces overall long-term correlation to about 50% or less .

Fig.4 graphically shows the time series of annual indices of NAO (December to March index of the Winter-NAO based on the difference of normalized sea level pressure between Lisbon, Portugal and Stykkisholmur/Reykjavik, Iceland : normalization is used to avoid the series being dominated by the greater variability of the northern station.), data from 1864 has been taken from the website of Jim Hurrel (Cf. acknowledgement) . Tim Osborn's NAO Update shows data for 182 years whose analysis shows similar results about 61-year periodicity.

Krishnamurthy and Goswami have demonstrated the interdecadal relationship between ENSO and Indian Monsoon 4 . Ni

o-3  SST  is inversely related to India's rainfall, therefore we have inverted the phase of Ni

o-3 in Fig-5 in order to facilitateits better visual comparison with Indian rainfall (11-yr running mean of standard deviations). Barring some exceptions, Ni

o-3 and ISMR admirably follow each other for long periods . Hence there can be no doubt about a 61-year cycle in El Ni

o . Since pressure data for Darwin  also shows a similar 61-year cycle, ENSO can be said to observe this 61-year cycle too .

From the website

HYPERLINK "http://www.ncdc.noaa.gov/gcag/gcag.html#HERE"

http://www.ncdc.noaa.gov/gcag/gcag.html#HERE

one can get monthly global precipitation data during past century, which clearly shows a 61-year cycle between data of 1901-41 and 1962-2002, shown in Fig-7 . For 26 out of 40 years, we find waveform conformity for a 61-year cycle. We have reorganised all these global or Indian annual rainfall data in terms of climatic year beginning from April for reasons which will be elucidated in next section . Yet the findings shown in Fig-7 are not conclusive, because the actual amount of global rainfall is uncertain due to lack of information about rainfall over seas and uncertainties or gaps in data from ground stations .

In line with the reasults obtained by Delworth, Manabe and Stouffer showing that ocean-atmosphere interaction can produce a climatic oscillation with period of ~50 years, together with a 65-70 year oscillation of the global surface temperature found by Schlesinger and Ramankutty, Goswami had rightly put forth a hypothesis that the quasi-60 year oscillation of the Asian monsoon and ENSO was manifestation of a global coupled ocean-atmosphere mode of oscillation 1 . The simple method  of pattern-recognition in waveform used by us to demonstrate a 61-year cycle in global and Indian rainfall can be discovered in all major time-series of rainfall and its predictors, with identical results, which support Goswami's hypothesis , provided data are arranged according to climatic year and climatic meridians explained below .

Goswami et al have noted :

The quasi

60 year oscillation of the monsoon rainfall varies coherently not only with ENSO, but also with the multidecadal variability of SST over the North Atlantic basin

15. This Atlantic Multidecadal Oscillation is believed to have a period between 65-85 years, but its relations with ISMR and ENSO calls for a reappraisal of the method through which AMO period was determined, because ISMR, EN and Darwin Pressure have all revealed a 61-year cycle. Long term trend rate often leads to different conclusions about periodicities which we can show through examples. Let us examine the case of annual Indian rainfall depicted in Fig-8 , in which the annual Indian rainfall depicted in Fig-2 is again analysed for finding long-term moving averages . Many researchers have noted ~30-year  phases of above and  below  normal  performance of ISMR, but a closer scrutiny reveals that this generalisdation needs some qualification . The periodicity of this long-term cycle depends upon the numbers of years for which moving averages are taken. For a 40 and 30 year moving averages, we get a plus-40 year half-cycle followed with a quasi-30 years year half-cycle . For 20 year moving averages, we get a ~60 year full period. At 10 and lower numbers for years of moving averages, the picture gets more and more blurred, till we again get a 61-year cycle for biennial or annual indices. Moving averages of 4 or 5 to 30 and 40 years suggest a periodicity of anything between 60 and 85 years in annual Indian rainfall , as was noted in the case of  AMO  or global surface temperature found by reseachers . But divergence from normal is so small in magnitude that this long-period term, after a ferw centuries when sufficient amount of data accumulates, may eventually turn out to be no different from the 61-year period so emphatically suggested by annual Indian rainfall and correlated indices .

During periods of weak coupling between predictors and ISMR, prediction becomes difficult and often impossible . Immediately after Walker's time, there was a long spell of unpredictable period in ISMR which can be termed chaotic, esp. 1935-1956 as evident in Fig-2 , and a long period after 2007 is expected by us to be highly chaotic. If indices of any period are used to make predictions for a chaotic period, good results cannot be obtained . Values of CCs are relative to the length and position of data window in the time domain . Modelers have sometimes to tailor their training periods and data windows so as to make forecasting feasible . But this selectivity is not recommendable. Unfortunately, there was no alternative . For the chaotic period, all methods fail to deliver desired results, including the spectral comparison with annual rainfall of 61-year ago as shown above.

We must try to unravel the factors behind this mysterious replication of waveforms after 61 years, which is essential if we want to discover a reliable long term forecasting technique. Chaos theory suggests that models based upon physical attributes of atmosphere cannot give a reliable forecast beyond nearly two weeks . Regularity in apparently chaotic climatic systems appearing at intervals of 61 years must be related to factors other than what have preoccupied the attention of researchers so far, although the presence of this  periodic regularity has long been recognized . The chaos theory is fit for physical systems related to monsoons, but the fundamental factors which regulate rainfall are not chaotic, otherwise waveform replication for decades would not have occurred at all . What is needed is a thorough re-examination of data with new and broader points of view .

2.  CLIMATIC  YEAR

Many researchers have thrown light on monthly variations in CCs .  Yasunari put forth the idea of a Monsoon Year starting just before the northern summer monsoon season 16   and argued :

(this) climatic year in the tropics is physically based upon the characteristic nature of the coupled ocean/land/atmosphere system over the Asian monsoon/Pacific Ocean sector

.  Yasunari estimated maximum CC of  Pacific SST with ISMR in January-February following ISMR in June-September, which implies a delinking and relinking somewhere between March and May . Gershunov et al 25 show monthly plot of El Ni

o correlation with ISMR during 124 years, with most negative value during Aug-Nov , which remains strong till March and starts strengthening again negatively from June owards . Hence, April-May is the weakest period when affinity of ISMR and Ni

o-3.4  (SST averaged over 5

N, 170

W) gets delinked. Therefore, the monsoon year must begin somewhere around April-May 20. Kripalani also notes :

the inter-connections between rainfall over South and East Asia as well as  circulation in Northern Hemisphere and tropical Pacific  appear to strengthen (or weaken) around the same time, implying that the monsoon related events over geographically separated regions seem to get linked (or delinked) around the same time

14 .

We arrived at the same conclusion from internal analysis of Indian rainfall data , without any consideration of any predictor . Fig-2 have data belonging to year beginning from April , while Fig-9 shows data in calendar year beggining from January. Even a visual comparison of Fig-9 with the predictable phase in Fig-2 belonging to same period shows that year beginning from April reduces the overall difference between both spectra 61 years apart . From January to May, we assumed five types of years , each beginning with a particular month . For all years beginning with January during past 136 years , standard deviation of differences in total annual rainfall of a given year from that of 61 year back was computed . Similarly,  standard deviations for years beginning with February, March, April and May were also computed  . Result, in percent variance from normal rainfall, is as follows : for years beginning with January standard deviation = 9.835 %, for February 9.39 % , for March 9.04 % , for April 9.22 % , for May 9.83 % were found . Fig-10 graphically shows the trend, which implies that if the monsoon year is supposed to start somewhere around late March or early April, the 61-year cycle discussed above would show minimum possible variance . Gershunov

s  monthly plot of El Ni

o correlation with ISMR shows that CC remains strong till March, hence this monsoon year is most likely to begin in April when old linkages break down and relinking begins to takes shape thereafter . Delinking of  ISMR and Ni

o-3.4  takes place around April-May . But 61-year cycle gives more weight to March-April . Mid-April seems to be the beginning of Climatic Year, when teleconnections of past year get delinked throughout the world, and a thorough reorganisation takes place for another year. It seems strange , but it is so . More explicit evidence for this conclusion, however, comes not from the linkages of teleconnections but from the analysis of global distribution of relative rainfall . To call it monsoon year is to reduce its significance , because directly or indirectly almost all major climatic phenomena of the world are interlinked , and monsoons are among the strongest elements in this system . Theoretically, vernal equinox ought to be related to monsoons , because monsoons result from land-sea contrast generated from solar heating . But few weeks are needed for the climatic transition to take effect . Year beginning from mid-April is the nearest approximation to this climatic year.

B N Goswami rightly says

The predictability of the seasonal mean monsoon depends on the nature of the interannual variability (IAV) of the monsoon

1  . Our study suggests that the interannual variability of the monsoon points towards some annually occurring obscure phenomenon in April which affects the course of rainfall throughout the year and therefore determines the overall annual predictability of monsoons and all other correlated phenomena. This obscure phenomenon is related to what was termed as a manifestation of

an internal oscillation of the coupled ocean atmosphere system

by Goswami et al 15, but this phenomenon is primarily annual in nature which was so convincingly evinced by waveform replication , and oscillations at intervals of 61 years are mere stochastic manifestations of this annual phenomena . Annual rainfall data arranged according to climatic year is best suited for unravelling the 61-year cycle , while seasonal ISMR data or data arranged according to Gregorian year blurs the picture . Spectral comparison of biennial Indian rainfall data yields a lesser conformity than an annual comparison . As we start increasing the number of years for taking moving averages, conformity with waveforms of 61-years ago start to decrease gradually. Moreover, delinking and relinking of teleconnections is also an annual phenomena .

Therefore,we may conclude that some mysterious event occurs at the onset of each climatic year which affects the overall performance of the whole climatic year , proven by replication of annual rainfall waveform after 61 years . This is the most important conclusion from the discovery and analysis of 61-year cycle . Quasi-biennial or any other oscillation is not intrinsically related to this 61-year cycle, because 61 is a prime number . Therefore, this long-term climatic periodicity proves that some unknown annual phenomenon in April regulates the performance of global rain making systems . This annual phenomenon must be related in some way to the apparent orbit of the Sun round our planet, because this is the very meaning of the term annual . The 61-year cycle, therefore, cannot be explained merely on the basis of thermodynamics alone, because due to the nonlinear rules of fluid dynamics governing Earth's atmosphere, reliable forecasts of the weather may not be possible for future periods beyond nearly 10-15 days, as Edward Lorenz had demonstrated over four decades ago. We must broaden our scope . If  physical conditions of April could not predict the rainfall of June in 1997 and 2002, how could the rainfall spectrum of 61 years ago could be copied during these years  ? Obviously, known physical predictors like temperature or pressure themselves are subservient to same hidden predictors which lie at the root cause of this mysterious 61-year cycle .

Sir Gilbert Walker worked in a period which coincided with the predictable phase of the 61-year cycle. When the chaotic phase set in (1935-1956), monsoon forecasters were so confounded with continuing failures that there was a prolonged inactivity in this field . The present generation of forecasters is lucky enough to have grown up and worked during a prolonged predictabnle phase, and therefore there is much optimism in their views . It is anybody's guess how the modelers would cope with the chaotic phase lasting a few decades which may visit us soon and may defy all attempts of finding out workable predictions, unles some novel technique for extended range forecasting is worked out .

3. CLIMATIC   POLES  AND  MERIDIANS

Waveform replication after 61 years suggests some annually ocurring phenomenon in April, which can be properly understood only in terms of the notion of a global climatic dipole . Only a brief outline of this concept can be presented here, because this concept is too complex and vast to be presented in a single paper . A fully functional model based of this dipole theory has already been finalized which is capable of making rain forecasts for all major regions of the world well in advance . The concept of global climatic dipole can be proven on the basis of global mapping of long-term relative rainfall , but annual shifts in climatic latitudes make this task difficult on a year to year basis .

Long-term comparison of annual relative rainfall over big chunks of land and geographical distribution of relatively strong and well established teleconnections suggest a global climatic dipole whose latitudes appear as crescent like regions of correlated phenomena upon a mercator map as shown in Fig-11 . Geographical distribution of annual relative rainfall can be adequately explained if we visualize Mt Kenya as climatic North Pole and central Pacific as Climatic South Pole, and draw the Climatic Meridians accordingly. Tropical rains , result from land-sea heat contrast, and therefore it is logical that they should be related to overall global land-sea distribution especially along the tropics . Central Pacific is the centre of biggest concentration of water, and on its opposite side we find Mt Kenya, which is the highest mountain on equator in a region nearest to the centre of the biggest landmass (Afro-Eurasia).  Whatever data has been analysed to date by us corroborates the idea of this global climatic dipole . But rainfall data in all regions of the world must be reorganized according to climatic year for making a fruitful investigation .

Bin Wang and Zhen Fan write

The equatorial western Pacific winds exhibit a considerably higher correlation with the ISM convection than with the Philippine convection

10. Kripalani and Kulkarni also conclude that the rainfall variations over north China are in-phase with South Asian rainfall 8. Like India, most of China is inversely related to El Ni

o as well . Positive Correlation of this region with West Pacific is also well known. Thus, most of India, China and mid-western Pacific form a crescent like region on a mercator map within which relative rainfall of any large area is generally in phase with that of other large areas of this region depicted as 4b in Fig-11 . A comparison of Indian and Chinese annual relative rainfall confirmed this homogeneity in a large number of cases ; during 66 out of 102 years shape of both waveforms also tallied (Cf. Fig-13; a better conformity will be seen if southernmost India is omitted which does not fit into rest of India as well; rainfall data for China is from

HYPERLINK "http://www.ncdc.noaa.gov/gcag/gcag.html"

http://www.ncdc.noaa.gov/gcag/gcag.html

which is not reliable) .

The correlation of Ni

o-3 with rainfall anomalies in the northeast Brazil shows multidecadal swings of weak and strong coupling similar to that with Indian Monsoon, which suggests an out of phase relationship of relative rainfall in India with that in north Brazil , especially during a large number of extreme cases . In 1997, India was expected to be drier while equatorial West Africa was expected to be wetter on account of increased Ni

o-3 activity,  but Ni

o-3 failed to influence both India as well as West Africa , while relative rainfall in India and West Africa maintained the inverse relation to each other even in 1997 ! Profound influence of Atlantic SST upon West Africa and northeast South America is a well known phenomenon. Hence, northern South America, mid-Atlantic and equatorial West Africa form a homogenous zone which is often but not always out of phase with respect to India in terms of relative rainfall. This zone has been depicted as 4a in Fig-11 due to its inverse relation with relative rainfall with 4b. This inverse relationship between climatic zones 4a and 4b is rather weak, and it should be studied in relation to another phenomenon : the magnitude of relative rainfall within a climatic zone is not homogenous throughout that zone, and generally shows a increasing or decreasing gradient of relative rainfall along climatic longitudes . Overall situation, therefore, is very complex and difficult to examine .

Regions like extreme southern India and Sri Lanka generally do not follow the rainfall pattern of rest of India and fall in the zone 5b (Fig-11).  Zones 3a and 3b, 4a and 4b, 5a and 5b, 6a and 6b form contrasting pairs characterized by a hitherto unnoticed phenomena : relative rainfall in 3a or 4a or 5a or 6a is inversely related to that in 3b or 4b or 5b or 6b respectively  in a large number of years, especially during extreme cases . But zones 1, 2 and 7 do not have any contrasting zone. Much of Western Europe falling in zone-2, for instance  does not have any positive or negative affinity with ENSO in this scheme, but is related to North Atlantic, as well as to regions from northeast  North America to California and mid-eastern Pacific. In a climatic hemisphere east or west of Mt Kenya, regions represented with positively correlated teleconnections are shaded in same colours (Fig-11). For instance, most of India, China, and a big chunk of Western Pacific is shaded in blue. But these regions are negatively correlated to regions of same colour-shading in the other climatic hemisphere. For instance, India or China is negatively correlated to equatorial regions west of Mt Kenya which includes the Ni

o near Peru.

Besides East Africa, Pacific is the most complex region, because it includes all these eleven basic climatic zones and various parts of the Pacific are positively or negatively correlated to all parts of the world and need accurate mapping of climatic events . For instance, northen Pacific is correlated to Red Sea or most of Europe except West Europe .

Three points must be borne in mind while examining this scheme. Firstly, comparison of relative rainfall indices over wide areas is the primary principle behind this pattern. Secondly, global multidecadal oscillation, especially the 61-year cycle, is often used to discover or invent such teleconnections which do not maintain strong correlations for a considerable stretch of time. The overall situation is highly complex, and therefore one should not hurry to any conclusion . Thirdly, most of global and major regional teleconnections are adequately explained by this scheme, but many climatic phenomena are often identified with regions which include more than one zone, and therefore blur the overall picture mostly on account of fluctuations in climatic latitudes which is an annual phenomenon occurring in April .

These climatic latitudes are not fixed geographically . At the beginning of each climatic year, an abrupt reshuffle of climatic latitudes occurs, which causes a variation of less than

( -8.0

in 2006-7, -4.83

in 2007-8, year beginning from mid-April), climatic longitudes remaining unchanged always. This abrupt annual variation in mid-April is the sole cause behind secular changes in correlation coefficients(CCs) and delinking or relinking of global teleconnections . The magnitude and direction of this mysterious annual shift in climatic latitudes can be precisely predicted much in advance, which may perhaps facilitate the task of updating CCs in all physical models.

Significance of the connections of equatorial bulge in Africa with gyroscopic regulation of earth

s motions has been known since the days of Newton, but its connections with periodicities in climatic penomena has never been properly investigated. The near-omnipresence of 61-year periodicity in mutually correlated as well as unrelated climatic phenomema often leads to discoveries of fake teleconnections, which have to be discarded later when they fail to deliver goods . This problem will make the task complicated . Hence we suggest a long-term global mapping and analysis of relative rainfall alone for examining the proposed notions of climatic latitudes and their annual shifts. This scheme contains many other important details which may be investigated later.

Fig-12 depicts a long term average of the extended-zone 4b comprising of most of India and China besides mid northwest Pacific regions, overlapping northern parts of the zone 5b. Region of maximum probability of climatic events for a climatic zone lies along the central climatic-latitude of a zone and the region of minimum probability lies 31

above and below this central latidude (Fig-7) . Magnitude of average rainfall within a zone generally varies linearly along the direction of climatic equator . But the overall quality of climate within a particular zone remains the same for the entire zone, with notable longitudinal differences which can be accounted for according to a different set of rules which have not been adequately codified. This global model has been tested for India for 1871-2006, without a single serious error, and many random tests have been carried out for other countries. Verification of annual or fortnightly forecasts generated from this model need data reprocessing according to the aforementioned grids for wanted periods, which is a time-consuming job. District-level variations cannot be predicted with reliable accuracy at present .

Thus, instead of varying the CCs of predictors, climatic grid-latitudes are made variable in our model. Annual global forecasts are made with reference to this flexible grid. But quasi-fortnightly forecasts for South Asia are made from a different grid, with centre on the Tropic of Cancer near Bhopal in India. Intraseasonal oscillations (ISOs) are an integral part of the Asian monsoon. The Indian summer monsoon is characterized by a quasi-biweekly oscillation whose basics have been captured in our quasi-biweekly forecasting model, and details of this scheme will be explained in future, which is as important as the concept of global climatic dipole.Before any attempt to unravel its physics we ought to re-analyse all the data properly and thoroughly . If we pay more attention to a thorough examination of raw data of relative rainfall in order to find hidden patterns of nature, a whole new world may be opened, literally, because this new approach is based upon the theory of two universes . This second universe is not composed of visible matter but influences the events of our universe profoundly . Surprisingly, rainfall to rainfall correlation among various regions within a climatic zone is much more strong and persistent than predictor-rainfall correlations , and is fully operative even during chaotic phases .

APPENDIX  ( may be omitted from this paper)  :

Annual global rainfall forecast for the year beginning from mid-April 2007 has been attached to this paper as appendicx, which was prepared and circulated in second half of April ,2007.

ANNUAL   FORECAST   OF  RELATIVE   RAINFALL  IN  SOUTH ASIA

During    2007-8 Climatic Year beginning From Mid-April

The Climatic Year 2007-8 beginning from mid-April will witness normall rainfall during the whole year, just 2% below normal,with

2% uncertainty, in India . But there will be marked regional differences, indicated in the table below. 2008-9 will see excess rainfall in India ( and China ), which will be surpassed only by the rainfall in the region from equatorial Africa west of Kenya to equatorial South America,both zones will see more rains in the north . India and China will face drought in 2009, more severe in south . 2010 will witness deficit in whole of India and China .

During 2007-8, South Asia has been divided into 18 zones for the sake of simplicity of presentation. The region comprising ten zones 16-18 and 1-7 is bifurcated by the thick line Line

(from NW border of Kutch to NW border of Nepal). Each of these ten zones will experience more rains to the NW of Line

than to the SE of it. These 10 zones,therefore, can be said to represent 20 zones really. The entire subcontinent  from Line

to the bottom of the map is bifurcated by Line

which represents the region of maximum rainfall in this belt. Line

and Line

are climatic latitudes running from Mt Kenya to Mid-Pacific.

Overall climatic conditions(esp. rainfall) in individual zones is shown as percents of gradient. These climatic conditions are explained in the table below. Relative rainfall of a zone is the average rainfall for that particular Climatic Latitude, but there may be marked differences at district level. Some of these finer details are also narrated in the table. These forecasts apply to the average of entire zone. Considerable differences from the average may be found at district or lower levels. Quasi-fortnightly forecasts for three months were distributed to hundreds of meteorologists in February 2007 for review.

Zone

Rainfall

No rains,esp in the west.Considerable losses due to drought in the belt south of Dehradun.

Conditions in eastern parts of this zone will be same as those in Zone 1 . But in western parts many regions will receive normal or excessive rains. Untimely rains resulting in losses, more pronounced in Hariyana, esp in Hissar, NE Punjab,etc.

Moderately good rains with in regions of high gradient.Little losses in low gradient areas. Little losses in low gradient areas in spite of deficit rains.

Same  as in Zone-3, but direction of gradient is reversed.

Two mutually contradictory predictors oppose each other, but one predictor is dominant, hence good rains proportional to gradient.

Same  as in Zone-3, but direction of gradient is reversed. Highest rains along Jodhpur-Vidisha line. Special treat for Ajmer and possibly Udaypur.

Scarcity in north, slight excess in south . Ahmedabad should get better rains than surroundings.

Indeterminate conditions. Chances of higher rains with along Mumbai-Bhopal line. Less than normal rains in northern portions of the zone, special conditions for Surat differing from surroundings.

Less than normal rains, proportional to gradient.

Similar to Zone-9, proportional to gradient.

Rains proportional to gradient. Normal or slightly more than normal near 100% gradient.

Same  as in Zone-11, but direction of gradient is reversed.

Excess rains proportional to gradient. Moderate storms.

Same  as in Zone-13, but direction of gradient is reversed.

Rains proportional to gradient, but not too much even in high gradient regions . Drought and losses in southern portion of this zone. Losses due to uneven rainfall in many districts.

Most excellent service of benevolent monsoon in the south of this zone(e.g. Varanasi,Gorakhpur,etc.), with decreasing effect nearly proportional to gradient.

17,18

Near normal rains, proportional to gradient. Losses in some regions.

Zone

Storms and Winds in 2007-8

Windy conditions in regions of higher gradient.

Windy conditions.

High winds and storms (not  destructive) in south.

Windy conditions along Mumbai-Bhopal line

High winds in high gradient areas.

( NOTE : Third section can be entirely omitted from this paper, together with figures 11 , 12 and 13 besides the appendix, because only a summary of important notions of the new theory has been outlined here. A fuller discussion requires separate works on each important idea mentioned briefly in Section-3.)

Maximum extents of two adjacent climatic extented- zones 4b and 5b, showing considerable overlapping over decades. The upper half of zone 4b is similarly overlapped by zone 3b, and the lower half of zone 5b by zone 6b . The long term probability of occurrence of a zone is maximum in the middle of these extended-zones.
